He never missed one back then. I believe that we both ended up there because we both, you know, hate to lose, and we both really want to win. And, you know, recently I came across this idea. There are athletes who simply want to win, and then there are some for whom victory isn't enough. They want domination. I believe that he is one of those as well. They want domination. And for that, what does a person have to do? They have to train. They have to grind morning and night. And there was this thing with us. After the morning, my father never let us sleep. When I say the morning, I mean after Fajr, the dawn prayer. He just wouldn't sleep himself, so we couldn't either. So every morning, six, seven, we'd be out doing our warm-up. Some people might call it a warm-up, but when you show up at the stadium in the morning, you run five kilometers, you do sprints, you work with resistance bands, you work on the pull-up bar, you do push-ups, dips, jumps, you know the bands then work with a partner, drilling and so on. That's about two hours of work. You drop two, two and a half kilos there. Not really a warm-up, but Father always called it that, right? That was a full morning training at seven AM. Then I came home to rest, and at four o'clock, we'd head to the gym. The training would start at five o'clock and end at seven. By eight, I'd be home, and by eleven, I'd rest up. And yet I kept thinking that something was missing. Like, I know that I have a start, a competition coming up in like forty or thirty days, and I would wake up and I'd be like, "Let me go to the sea. I'm gonna go down to the sea, do some running, some sprints on the sand, work out, and so on." And so many times, I ended up going out for a third training session as well. Sometimes Father would say, "Rest. In the morning, we're gonna have a heavy workload." But sometimes I wouldn't tell Father. I'd just go out at night, head out, and train. At night when everyone's asleep, when nobody's around, I'd grab one of my close friends, give them a call, have them pick me up, then hop in the car and head to the sea. I'd train for an hour or an hour and a half, come back, and crash. Next morning, I'd get up and start all over again. And just this routine, nonstop. And when I see guys, you know, when they do just one training session or like they say, "On Wednesday, we have a morning training, then an evening sauna session." What do you mean? You have to fight in fifty days. You have a huge fight ahead and you want to take a rest in the middle of the week? Well, rest on Sunday. On Saturday, do a morning training, hit the sauna in the evening, take a complete rest on Sunday, and start all over again on Monday. That was just my approach. Everyone does it differently. I don't know. I just think it has to be in your head. You've got to want it incredibly badly. - So you believe that to be a champion, you have to train like that, you have to be that serious for the majority of people? - Let's say I believe that to be at the top, the top tier, right? One of the best in the world. A person has to always be, you know, a little bit, let's say just a little bit crazy, but in a good sense of the word. You have to be addicted to it and to want this incredibly bad. You have to take a fanatical approach to the training process. Just showing up and working out, that's what hundreds of thousands of guys are doing around the world. To stand out from that, from this crowd, let's say, who have the exact same goals as you do, to stand out from that, you have to take a fanatical approach to it. - Well, look, I'm training with John Danaher. And they train like that too. They train three times a day. And it actually feels right this way. I mean, of course, this is how you have to train. - 'Cause of that, John has one of the best- - Yeah, he makes champions. - ... students in the world, and after all, they don't just take some random place. I mean, they're not somewhere in the middle. They're at the top, and look at their whole approach. Some people... Look, a person with that kind of approach just can't be somewhere in the middle. It's impossible. If you have a fanatical attitude, the right attitude toward the training process, on top of that, you have to be fanatical about it. You love what you do, and in the end, there will be results. Well, just look at John and look where his guys are. He creates champions. He finds a champion in everyone, across every category, right? - He creates champions, not just one or two, but all of them. He finds a champion in everyone, across every category. - Look, the people who come to him, he has a system, let's say, right? Under this roof, under his roof, there is a system. People come here, and they become champions in this system. So there are people there who are champions. These are specific established names. And there are other people who came and left. They entered that black room, so to say, an uncomfortable place. They went in, stood for a year, a year and a half or two, and left. Nobody talks about them. These are the people who came in, broke mentally, and left, and there aren't just a few of them. There are a lot. Usually, most people give up. There are many who give up, but then there are the ones who come in and stay there for a long time. Long, long time. And after some time, they achieve success. They become the best. They prove to themselves that once they enter the system, they could stay there for a long time, endure, and become the best. They prove that they are already capable of many things. They can be trusted to supervise big, important projects. I would even say, for example, 2,000 years ago, they could easily rule entire cities. So a person who has gone through it physically, who has lived through it, who has faced challenges, passed the tests, they are capable of a lot, highly capable. You don't just walk into martial arts and become the best. It's very difficult. It means that you go through pain, you make sacrifices, you give your time, you face setbacks and failures in training. The mental strength and capacity mean a lot there. And when I talk about his system, look, I mean those schools that became the best, let's say, in football or in freestyle wrestling or, let's say, in MMA, in grappling, or in judo. It's really hard to enter one of the best schools in the world, to stay when they are breaking you, throwing you, choking you, to improve as a person and as an athlete, and to become a champion. It takes at least 12 years, 10 to 12 years, maybe 15. For some, it took longer. And to get through the grind for these 15 years and then to break through and become the best is worth a lot. - Have you had those tough moments where you're almost broken psychologically? You know, just sitting there, finding it hard to breathe, and everything is going wrong, lost in some situations. And when you were young, was there ever a moment like that? 'Cause the guys from Dagestan on the team, really great guys, by the way, all say they've never seen you broken in training, not even losing really. But when you were young, did it ever happen? - Look, I've been in this grind since I was a kid. Wherever my father coached, there were always... Let's take the year 2004, for example. My father put together the Dagestan national team for combat Sambo and in mixed martial arts, which was called no-holds-barred fighting. There were training camps, too. I was 15. It was the winter of 2004, and I was the youngest guy there. The others were, let's say, between 22 and 30, all active world champions. At that time, I was, like, 14, 15 years old. I wrestled with them, grappled, was in this uncomfortable environment for myself, where we always had the rule that the youngest has to do everything, and he is always wrong, and I had to deal with that. Especially in the gym, they throw you, hit you again and again. You have to show up morning and evening, day after day. This place hardens you. They say How the steel was tempered. Steel isn't tempered for nothing. How many times do you have to strike the steel before it becomes a sword? That doesn't just happen by itself. - Did you believe in yourself then? - Some of the guys who were at those camps back then, they're grown men now. Even then, they were older than me by 10, 12, maybe 15 years. And they always used to say, "Khabib, we honestly thought you were going to become some kind of scholar or scientist." Because my father was always making me read and write, read and write. I would copy out entire books by hand. There was always this control. It wasn't like he'd say, "Khabib, go do this", and you could just walk away and not do it. When he said, "Go do it", you did it. My father never forgot anything. He had a phenomenal memory. He'd be like, "Did you do it?" And everything he told you, he remembered. He just did. - He checked you. - He was like, "Come here." And he'd check. I knew it wasn't something I could get away with. So, you know, since childhood, I was lucky that my father always kept me close, and I found myself in this environment among fighters who at that time- ... were, I don't know, they just didn't compete in America and Europe back then. But in the post-Soviet space, they were the best at that time. And it helped me a lot to become mentally strong. - Tell me more about your father. You've read a lot. You didn't just fight. You were well-rounded and you told several stories about this. There was a book, what is it called? Countries and Peoples. - Yes, from my childhood. - It is, really. - Yes. - It went on- - The approach was the same. - Tell that story because I think it's important for the parents listening to us to understand this. You're not just trying to create a champion. You're trying to raise a well-rounded person, a wise person, a person with depth. - Yes, you have to give the right motivation to kids with proper communication. My father was very good at this. I now realize interacting with my own kids how wise he was and how deeply he understood this. Because when a child is eight, 10 years old, and when they are just beginning to form as a person, it is important to have the right approach. I now understand how farsighted my father's approach was. First, he tried not to give me anything for free. Only sometimes I could get something for free. For example, before a holiday, like Eid al-Fitr or before school started, Father would give money. I'd go buy clothes at the market for back to school, that kind of thing, you know? And at other times, he always told me, "Well, if you want it, you need to earn it. You want me to give you money just like that?" There would be some work around the house and so on that I had to do. And I knew that encouragement would always be there. My father always had this kind of approach. He set a very strong personal example, very strong, and I really learnt a lot from him. Took a lot. And then, as you mentioned, there was the book, Countries and Peoples. Someone gave it to him as a gift. My father read through it, and he realized that it was perfect for me, for a child who was 10, 11, maybe 12 at the time. That book was part of my life for about two or three years, something like that. It was about 193 countries. The geography, history, all the details. For each country, it describes its geographical location, which countries it bordered, its major rivers, mountains, cities, history, economy, finances. Just brief information about every country. My father said, "You'll tell me about each country, and I'll give you money for it." And I thought, "All right." I knew this was like my own little capital just sitting there waiting for me. All I had to do was come, take that book, read it, understand it. Then my father would take the book and say, "Now, tell me what you read." And he would check. He would look through it. The countries were arranged alphabetically. A for Andorra, Angola, and so on. B for Belgium, Brazil, and so on. Everything went in alphabetical order. I started from A and read them all straight to the very end. Like every single time when I needed the money, I did that. And for example, I go over the first 40 countries, he'd check a box, and then I continued. That's how my father would test me. Let's just say that my knowledge of geography comes from back then. - Did you realize how big the world was? - No. - As a child, you don't understand? - At that time, I didn't understand. But since then, I've had this desire to travel. I really wanted to travel, visit different countries, see wonders of the world, the Colosseum, the pyramids. But all of this is from childhood, from what I read and wrote. It wasn't just about reading. I also had to rewrite it. I had to write it down. I always wrote down what I read. And actually, this is very good advice for parents, you know. They can use this with their children too. They can tell them to sit down and copy books by hand. First of all, eyesight improves. Reading improves. Writing, how you write improves. Your handwriting. And it stays with you when you read and write. It really develops a child. Not only I believe that, I actually do advise parents to motivate their kids this way. You can simply create some kind of challenge that children benefit from and reward them for it after it is done. This really helps develop a child's mind. - I actually saw advice from the great writers who said that they remembered doing the same thing when they were young. When they first started writing, they just simply copied other legendary writers. You read their work and then you type it out yourself, and you start absorbing that style into your bloodstream. - Well, it needs to be done in childhood. When the child is ready to learn and his mind is open, let's say as a metaphor, when his table is clean, not occupied with clutter, then as a father, you can help them place all the right things on the table in their proper place. Let's say put the food in one place, flowers in another, something like that. It is important. That's why I believe I was very lucky. My father did the right thing. He provided me with the right kind of information, and I learned a lot just from being around him. I learned so much just from his presence. He was there at home, and then we also went to competitions. He was always there at trainings and at camps, so I absorbed a lot from him, and it was interesting for me too. Of course, when you're a teenager, sometimes you're tired, sometimes you want to break free. All of that was there. But my father had the right approach. He influenced me a lot and in the right way. - And there's the physical component too of hard work. There's a story you told about the time he made you dig a big hole just because it was difficult. There's wisdom in that. That you just need to work. You need to stay busy all the time. - These were training camps from two thousand and five to two thousand and seven. In the summer, we went to the mountains. There was a children's camp there, an old Soviet-style pioneer camp where we stayed doing our runs in the forest. Together, we built a small training area there with a rope, a punching bag, a pull-up bar. We did sprints there. We pulled resistance bands. After running in the mountains, we would come back and work there. There were almost no mats. We built the base ourselves. It had a dining room on the first floor and an attic space where we slept. We would come downstairs, eat, go outside, train in that little sports area, then run in the forest. It was somewhere around twelve hundred meters above sea level, a conifer forest. We spent the whole summer there. There we'd train in the morning, then there was nothing to do all day, and in the evening we had training sessions again. But during the day, so we wouldn't be busy with unnecessary things, my father sometimes gave us work. And we'd ask, "Why are we digging this pit?" He'd say, "Well, people used to use it like a refrigerator. When you dig two or three meters down, it is cold inside. You can put milk there, cheese, things like that, so the food does not go bad." And we were like, "Okay, but do we really need these refrigerators every day?" So in that sense, this was his approach back then. He always had this kind of unique approach. Well, what's the philosophy here? That you gotta be busy all the time, working hard, doing something difficult. Yes, it's very dangerous to give a child free time in adolescence. You should constantly develop and improve. And by this time, you should already, I believe, have decided what you want to become. You should already be investing in what you want to turn into. By the way, you had several very good conversations with Nurlan, with Adam, which I highly recommend. You were kind of bigger, and he needed protection at that time. You were that protection. Can you tell us these stories? How often did you fight on the streets of Dagestan? Well, let's start with the fact that I never bullied anyone. Never. Although I physically could bully, yes, let's say I had the capability. I wasn't the kind of person who started the fights. You didn't start? I didn't. Well—Tried to finish. Well, maybe there were some moments, but mostly no. Mostly I didn't start. Abubakar, sure, he was like that. In my circle, I had some guys who were a little bit like that. He was a troublemaker. He was a troublemaker, and because of him, I got into trouble many times. When you're, let's say, fourteen, fifteen years old and your brother is fighting, you don't really ask who's right or who's wrong. You just go in, you start to, as we say, you interfere. You just have to go in, right? But when you're older, more conscious, when you're twenty, twenty-five or thirty years old, this is when you really start telling him, "You know, Abubakar, I think you're wrong here. Yes, we'll finish this right now, but in the future, I wouldn't want you to get involved because I think it's wrong, right?" Well, as kids, we just didn't ask. We just went right in. When we moved to Makhachkala in 1999, let's say the end of 1999, so there were—we were many brothers, more than ten of us. We lived in one house. Some were in school, some in college, some in university. But we all had one routine. We trained in the morning, then went to school. We'd come home, rest, do homework, then train in the evening, come home and rest, without mothers, just by ourselves. Father gathered everyone. He took in close friends and family, his nephews, cousins, relatives, those who didn't have a father, and so on. He took them in, and we all lived in one house. We cooked, cleaned, did laundry all by ourselves. It was like, well, some kind of army life, you know? And when the brothers had some problems, let's say misunderstandings, we didn't go easy on those guys. And even those who lived around there near that house, in that district, in the settlement, everyone knew that there were lots of brothers living there. So they had to be careful. And we had a football field there. It was right in front of the house, and everyone who came to play there, we constantly had fights there, misunderstandings. There were older brothers. They were always watching us, keeping us from trouble. "Don't do this. Don't do that. Watch your mouth." But we had our own little group. The age difference between us was maybe two, three years. There were five or six of us. We'd always go outside and play football. If our team was losing, then the game would just turn into a whole different sport, and that's how we were growing up. We spent six, seven years there, almost ten years. We were living together, growing, training. A lot has happened over that time, but I was never... Well, maybe just a couple of times, okay? Mostly, I was never the initiator of fights with some exceptions. I just remember since childhood, my father always told me, "You don't need to use force on the street." My father looked at all of this in a completely different way. What is your understanding of the difference between the street fight and sports? There is a very big difference. I saw many fights where if you put those same guys into sparring, in wrestling, MMA or combat Sambo, one guy could beat the other very easily. But the street is completely different. There are no rules, no referees, no points. You just fight there, and it's totally different. I can say I actually had a major turning point in my life. Let's say the one that, in terms of psychology, really changed my whole attitude towards MMA. Back in twenty oh two, twenty oh three, my father kept me in wrestling. He trained MMA combat Sambo, but wanted me to stay in wrestling. My cousin, who was older than me by a couple of months, he was training with my father. His freestyle wrestling was weak, so my father sent him to me, to the place where I was training. It was called the School of Higher Sports Mastery. The local guys from the city know this place. He came to practice and got into it with a guy in our gym who was heavier, maybe one class heavier. This guy was the best in his weight, winning Dagestan and the city too. So they got into a conflict. They were arguing. My brother had just joined. He was new to our freestyle wrestling gym, right? But I knew the other guy well. They paired up and they got into it. Well, that's my brother, right? So the guy goes, "Let's meet up after." And they agreed, as we say, to step outside after the training. I remember it was raining heavily, a real downpour. The guy brought his brother. I came with mine. We went outside and they started fighting one-on-one. I remember he shot in, lifted and took him down. Abdurakhman, my brother, threw up a triangle and he choked him out. When we went out, I was thinking Abdurakhman will lose to this guy. I even visualized it happening, the whole thing. Like, this guy beats him, and then I start fighting. Well, I'm not going there for my brother to lose. In my head, I was getting into a fight. But he threw him locked up in a triangle. For the first time I saw with my own eyes how someone chokes another person with a triangle and they lose consciousness. Wow. They fought, he put him to sleep. We had to pull them apart, me and other guys. Fights over, Abdurrahman won. And on the way home, I'm like, "Abdurrahman, wow. You impress me." I came home and immediately told my father. I said... That was in 2002 or '03, if I'm not mistaken. I am 14 years old. I said, "I'm switching to combat Sambo." I said, "What Abdurrahman did, the one he choked out, he's not just anyone in the gym. He's one of the best wrestlers, but he couldn't do anything." My father said, "You'll go to wrestling, but you'll come to me, I'll teach you. You'll learn techniques here and so on. But I want you to gain wrestling skills." I started going three days to train with my father, three days to wrestling. And at that moment, I realized that no matter how strong the wrestler is, lying on the back is where the wrestling normally ends, but MMA only begins there. And I was like, I thought, "I need this. I need this mixed style." I realized this was for me. I started going to my father's trainings, despite that he didn't let me. He didn't want me to do fighting. I think most people don't know this. My father wanted me to do freestyle wrestling or judo, but in my head, I already had this mixed style. I always wanted to compete. I was already getting tapes to watch. I always wanted to compete where there are no rules, where I could fully use my skills. Maximum freedom. Yes, judo, Sambo, freestyle, grappling, jujitsu. More people knew jujitsu than grappling. After that, I switched, and from there my journey slowly started, which I had already begun. That's when I started the amateur journey. Then when I started getting good results as an amateur, I realized that I can switch to pro and achieve success. Sambo. In combat Sambo. Combat Sambo. I competed in all kinds of sports. Everything that involves wrestling and fighting. I competed everywhere. I competed in freestyle, in judo, in grappling, in combat Sambo. I competed in sport Sambo, in hand-to-hand combat, in pankration. My father put me in all competitions. He would say, "You have a competition here and another one there. Now you are preparing for this one. You will compete in this weight, under these rules, in this sport." And he was sending me everywhere. He would tell me, "You will need this in the future. The more competition experience you have, the more relaxed and confident you'll feel. It builds up over time." When I was young, I was losing as well. I go to a judo competition, win two matches and lose two matches. I would ask, "Father, why are you sending me there? This is not my sport." And he'd say, "No, go and compete." So, okay, I went, I cut weight. The next day you come in, you see 50 competitors in your weight class. You let this weight, this competitive energy pass through you, and psychologically you get better, better, better each time. You become more relaxed about it. And this moment really affected me, although then I didn't agree with my father. Back then I thought, "Why should I compete in other sports?" He said, "You can wrestle, right? Well, go wrestle." I was saying, "But judo isn't my sport. It's hard for me there." He says, "Well, you went on four times. Look, you won twice, then lost twice. You competed four times. You're 15 years old. You're just developing. Do you know how valuable this experience is for you? Four matches in one day." Now when I'm coaching, I understand the psychological approach, how important it is. I realize now how correctly he prepared me for the big stage. The preparation was already happening then when I was 15 years old. For example, when I went into the biggest fight in combat sports history, when I fought in 2018 here in Las Vegas. At that point, I was already at my peak, prime time. I was 30 years old. I went through all possible competitions as an amateur and a professional. And you know what? I was ready. This preparation, it started back then when I was 15. These are things that are inside all of us. The real question is how you deal with them, how you overcome them within yourself. 100% you can't ignore it, and you must work through it. You have to answer the questions, "Why did this happen? Could I do it this way instead?" And this comes only with experience, only with experience. And the more you compete, you know, the more you train and the more you face it. Some people actually try to avoid that moment. But the more you face it, the better and better you'll become each time. It's the same with athletes. They get nervous. That's what we call the jitters, right? At first, whenever someone competes, they get the jitters. But when you keep competing over and over again, you gradually learn how to channel those nerves in the right direction. Then it gets easier for you. And in order to get there, you have to compete. And what is competition? You meet this fear face to face. You don't avoid it. A lot of guys are staying away from competitions. When they're young, they have this tension, this self-consciousness, this fear, the jitters. They're nervous. They can't sleep at night. You have to go through all of that yourself. Nobody can do it for you. A coach can train you. A coach can teach you. But all he can say is, "Practice, practice, practice." Then there's you, alone with your own feelings. When you lie down, when you wake up, when you walk, when you're by yourself, when you eat, there are so many moments when a person has to go through all of this on his own. And when you have to go through this over and over again, some people back away. They don't want to face it. And what do you need for that? Patience. You have to come and endure it and go through all of it, through your skin, through your muscles, through your mind. You must let all of this pass through you and overcome it. And each time you'll get better at it. A good example of that is the third person you mentioned, George St-Pierre, who by the way sends his regards. I train with him a lot, and he has a very deep respect for you. But he didn't give me much good advice because when I told him I was going to train with you, he told me... What did he say? He texted me, "If you die, you die." That's what he said. Good advice. He's a wise man. Didn't support me and said, "Say hi." Tell him I say hi, too. As we were talking about, he himself has spoken about this, that he was afraid and always felt fear before every single fight. That's interesting. One of the greatest fighters—In history, yeah. And even he feels fear. He's not afraid to admit it. Everybody has fear. Everybody gets afraid. But I don't know how big that fear was for him. I really don't know. We never discussed it. But I've watched a lot of his interviews, his fights, and his training camps. The way he analyzed his past fights and all that, the way I see it, he's a very sensitive person. There are people like that too. Some guys take all of this very lightly. Like, "Okay, we fight tomorrow. We'll see who my opponent is and how he shows up, right?" But there are others who, who find it very difficult to handle psychologically. But the Almighty gave me this kind of calmness. Sometimes I'd get to the arena, and they bring you in four hours before the fight. They have to weigh you, they have to do doping control, and they have to tape you up. There are so many athletic commission procedures. Every state has different rules, how they bring you into the arena and all that. Sometimes I'd get there four hours early and think, "What am I supposed to do?" So I make myself a little bed and say, "Please, keep it quiet." I would lie down. I could sleep there for an hour, maybe an hour and a half. That's it. Wake up, warm up, and then I could go out and fight. And some guys, they don't sleep for two days before the fight. Believe me, there are fighters who don't sleep the night before a fight, and it's very hard on them. It's not really fear. It's hard to describe with just one emotion. It's too many emotions that come over a person, and they can't fall asleep. It's panic, it's fear, it's worry, it's nervousness, it's the jitters. All of that builds up, and the person just can't fall asleep. So in that regard, the Almighty gave me a little more calmness than others. I had, you know, such a cool head and calmness before the fights. Post-Training Is How You Keep Your Taste | Fireworks CEO Lin Qiao

Sequoia Capital(236.0K)·28:29·4.4K views·2026-08-12
Lin QiaoCo-founder & CEO, Fireworks AI
TL;DRPost-training allows companies to embed their unique judgment and taste into AI models after achieving product-market fit, serving as both a strategic moat and a 5–10x cost reduction mechanism for production serving.
Evolution of Application Development: Software development...

TL;DR

Post-training allows companies to embed their unique judgment and taste into AI models after achieving product-market fit, serving as both a strategic moat and a 5–10x cost reduction mechanism for production serving.

Industry Context & The Shift Toward Owning Intelligence

  • Evolution of Application Development: Software development has collapsed from taking tens of product engineers and PMs across multiple quarters to a single person building in a few weeks without writing a single line of code, shifting focus from off-the-shelf black-box APIs to building deeper moats.
  • The Value of "Taste": Every company exists to solve unique problems with its own judgment, taste, and conviction; building on generic APIs risks losing that differentiation.
  • What "Owning Your Intelligence" Means:
    • Data: Curing production data with high quality and generating synthetic data to enrich it.
    • Model Weights: Building on top of existing models and owning the weights using tailored, interoperable techniques.
    • Serving & Optimization: Running AB testing to verify product metrics, then feeding insights back into the loop.
  • Audience Breakdown & Engagement Models:
    • *Deep Researchers (e.g., Cursor, Cognition):* Want extreme results and control over every knob, using lowest-level APIs with direct RL rollouts and fully controlled trainers.
    • *Product/ML Engineers:* Want high-level control via training SDKs and close iteration with Fireworks' researchers.

The Post-Training Progression

  • Phase 1: Prompting – Few-shot prompts used as-is for minutes of interaction to quickly test ideas.
  • Phase 2: RAG & Context Engineering – Grounding AI usage into your own data for hours of interaction; ideal when factual data is dynamic and constantly changing.
  • Phase 3: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) – Taking a few hours of data to reflect personalized taste, product choice, or correct structural/behavioral output issues.
  • Phase 4: Preference Tuning (DPO) – Collecting user interaction data (thumbs-up, thumbs-down) to help models learn product taste and user preferences.
  • Phase 5: Reinforcement Learning (RL) – Building domain expertise (legal, finance, healthcare, customer support, recruiting, marketing, sales) when models are weak on specific, complex problems.
  • Phase 6: Distillation – Training a smaller student model via a teacher model to make serving faster and 5–10x more economical (applicable to LLMs, VLMs, and image generation models).

Common Pitfalls in Post-Training

  • Data Quantity vs. Quality: Throwing raw data at training does not guarantee great outcomes; data quality must be controlled, with the product team acting as the ultimate judge of quality.
  • Vibe Evals vs. Systematic Evals: Relying on "vibe checking" founder sentiment instead of systematic unit and integration tests (similar to traditional software development).
  • Sloppy RL Environments: Building simulations that fail to reflect reality, leading models to hill-climb on bad simulations.
  • Reward Hacking: Models finding unintended shortcuts to meet optimization goals (e.g., generating zero lines of code to eliminate compilation errors).
  • Training-to-Serving Stack Mismatch: Quality drops when moving from a training stack to a serving stack if libraries, numerics, and optimizations differ, causing a loss of precision.

Real-World Examples & Use Cases

  • Cursor: Started mid-to-post training early last year to control model supply and customer preference data, successfully competing at frontier quality on par with frontier labs.
  • Doximity: Uses Clinic AI on Fireworks for deep medical questions, topping the Stanford-Harvard clinic safety benchmark.
  • Factory: Fine-tuned a model on Fireworks focused on coding security, topping security benchmarks where tolerance for error is extremely low.
  • GenSpark: Built a general-purpose co-working application for deep professional research and slide generation that competes with frontier models while achieving a 5–10x cost reduction.
  • Target Segments:
    • *Frontier Agent Builders:* Build bespoke customized harness systems co-optimized with custom tools via post-training.
    • *Incumbents:* CFOs block AI feature launches due to massive traffic cost burdens; post-training prevents scaling into bankruptcy.
    • *Specialized Model Operators:* Cutting-edge developers performing significant post-training.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Lin Qiao predicts that in the future there will be millions of specialized models, amounting to "one application per use case."

Notable Quotes

"If you build on top of off-the-shelf API, then you really need to think about how you keep that special taste judgment and unique part forward." — Lin Qiao
"We have been asking a model to generate to do this is coding example to create code that minimize the compilation error. Okay, so guess what the model did? The model generate zero line of code... This is a kind of one example of reward hacking." — Lin Qiao
"Product market fit doesn't really mean you have a durable scalable business... only after you hit the product market fit, the data you collect from product surface area are really meaningful." — Lin Qiao

The fun part of my job is that we get to see a lot of patterns regarding the innovations people are building on top of us, the challenges they are facing, and the trends developers are following. In the past year, software and application development have been somewhat disrupted. In the past, if you had a good idea and wanted to implement it and scale to production, it required a team of tens of very strong product engineers and PMs working together for multiple quarters to deliver that. Right now, with one person and a few weeks, without understanding how to write a single line of code, you can do that. That collapsing of resources required—both in terms of timeline and deep expertise—is shifting how competitive the application space is. It is shifting people from thinking about building on top of off-the-shelf black box APIs to building a much deeper moat so that they can build a more durable business. As you have seen, there is a lot of discussion, especially in the past week, between open models and closed models, and all the rallying and support across the open model space. The depth of that alliance exists because we believe—I mean, the industry is much deeper because look at the entire industry; there are so many companies. All of you are building your own companies. Every single company exists for a reason because they focus on solving a unique problem in a special way. That means they carry their own judgment, taste, and determination and conviction into that product, and that is why a company exists. Today, if you build on top of an off-the-shelf API, you really need to think about how you keep that special taste, judgment, and unique part forward. We believe one approach for every company to build a durable business is to actually bake your judgment, taste, and deep customer understanding into the intelligence you build on top of, instead of just using an off-the-shelf API. That is the context of what is happening in the industry and why post-training could be very relevant to you. You have probably heard a lot about owning intelligence rather than renting it. What does owning your own intelligence mean? It actually means many things. First of all, it starts with data. Intelligence is a derivative of data, and obviously, the foundation labs and all the foundation models we are using are building on top of public data and labeled data that has solved common tasks. But all of you are solving a specific task; that is why you are building a business and a company. Being able to curate production data with high quality and even generate synthetic data to enrich your production data is one step toward owning your own intelligence. After you have data, you will start to use that data, turn it into a model, build on top of an existing model, and own the weights. There is a collection of techniques you can use to get there. Those techniques are tailored to solve different kinds of problems you could possibly have, and those techniques can also interoperate with each other for you to reach your final goal. After you have a great model belonging to yourself solving your specific problem really well, you need to work on serving it. You will probably first do some A/B testing to make sure it really moves the needle for your product metrics, and then go back into this loop. I don’t think you should jump into post-training right away; there are different phases you will go through. First, everyone starts with prompts and uses the model as is. It is few-shot. Quickly, you can use that to test your ideas. Then, you use RAG to ground the usage of AI into your own data, and you can start to do a lot of context engineering from that. Those interactions go from minutes to hours. Then, you progress into, "Hey, I have some data; I want to see how my data is going to reflect and make the model work better with my product." So, you will start to do supervised fine-tuning, which will take you a few hours. Then you reach, "Hey, I want the model to reflect personalized taste; it is a very unique choice for my product." So, you want to start using preference information collected from user interactions—thumbs-up, thumbs-down—to help the model learn your product taste. Finally, you want to build a model that carries your expertise in that domain, whether that expertise is in legal, finance, healthcare, customer support, recruiting, marketing, or sales. Even in one industry vertical, there are so many subdomains. All of that is unique and special towards the product you are building. Usually, you have heard a lot about reinforcement learning, which is used to build toward a specialty. This progression is very similar to how we human beings learn knowledge over time. For example, we learn a lot of knowledge by reading literature. In the literature, it says what is correct and what is not correct; this is very similar to supervised fine-tuning. As we grow, we develop our own taste and judgment of how we want to conduct a specific way of approaching a problem, and that is preference tuning or DPO. Over the course of time, we learn to do a really good job in a certain area—whether I want to be an accountant and really know how to build into financial data, or I want to be a dentist and learn the details of how to operate with dentistry. All of this is exactly how we human beings acquire knowledge. Also, it is very interesting that different techniques are there to solve different kinds of problems. For example, if the model doesn't know a fact, and the fact is very dynamic—the facts of your data in your product are very dynamic—then typically you use RAG to solve that problem. However, if your model output’s behavior or structure is off, you curate data and do supervised fine-tuning to correct that. If the quality of the model’s answer is personal or specific to the taste of your product, then you use preference tuning. If the model is quite weak on the special problem you are trying to solve, then you use reinforcement learning. If the end result of the model is too slow or too expensive to serve in production, then you use distillation to let the teacher teach a much smaller student model so it can be more performant or economical. Obviously, distillation also means different things for LLMs, VLMs, or image generation models. If you are interested, we can talk more about that. There are many different ways teams start to try these technologies, and they may not be happy with the experience because they can burn money and time but may not reach their ideal results. Here are the areas where you can possibly feel frustrated. For example, when it comes to data—and data is the essence of tuning—quantity is not the most important thing. Actually, quality is the most important. But sometimes, just by throwing tons of data into a training process, you may not get a great outcome. So, you really need to control the data quality. Usually, who is the best judge of data quality? It is actually your product team. That is where we see the convergence of before GenAI, where the product team and the research or ML team were separate organizations. They were separate teams. Now, they work hand-in-hand to make things happen. These days, when people post-train GenAI models, we see the product team needs to make judgment calls on data quality and be deeply involved in the process so they can ensure the best result. The second thing is you are going to have evals. I know all of you are very busy and focused on launching quickly, and a lot of evals are just "vibe checks." The founders look at the result and feel, "Is it right or not?" Actually, this is judgment. This is you putting your judgment of the end result and deciding whether it is good or not. You must convert that judgment into systematic evaluation. This is no different from traditional software development, where you have unit tests and integration tests to ensure quality. Similarly, if you think about doing post-training, having a way to build evals and build your own judgment into a repeatable process is extremely important. Then there could be situations where, when you do RL, there could be a sloppy RL environment where you build a simulation, but the simulation is not really reflecting reality. Then the model can hill-climb on a bad simulation, and it could possibly do reward hacking and all kinds of weird stuff. A fun story about reward hacking is that we were asking a model—in this coding example—to generate code that minimizes compilation errors. Okay. So, guess what the model did? The model generated zero lines of code. Okay, there’s no compilation error, but that’s absolutely not what you want. This is one example of reward hacking. It is very common because the model is very smart; it will try in all different ways to get your goal, but it may not be what you want. Pay attention to all these details and try not to let the model outsmart you. Obviously, think about your development process as experimentation. Training the model is not the end of your experiment. The final judge is whether product metrics are moving or not. You need to bring the final model into a serving tier and do A/B testing. That transition is extremely important because the quality can drop if you move from one training stack to a serving stack without aligning the two. Think about the model as tons and tons of calculations, math, and matrix multiplications. The way to do matrix multiplication and calculation—if you use a different library, different numerics, and different optimization—will lead to different results. Therefore, the end result of a training may not be replicated, or you even lose precision during serving. That alignment is very important. I can give you more examples of that. There are a few other challenges you will run into, and I’m happy to talk with you more about them in detail offline. There have been many pioneers we have worked with on post-training. Baking your data, which reflects product engagement and all that deep knowledge, into your model is a way to preserve that. Second, you can post-train a model that brings down the cost five to ten times. RL Environments Explained: How AI Agents Learn Real-World Work | Brendan Foody, Mercor

Sequoia Capital(236.0K)·26:22·17.7K views·2026-08-12
Brendan FoodyCEO, Mercor
TL;DRBrendan Foody, CEO of Mercor, explains how Reinforcement Learning (RL) environments—comprising worlds, apps, and tasks—are replacing low-skilled crowdsourced data to train agentic AI models.
He highlights how this technology is shifting from frontier labs to application-layer...

TL;DR

Brendan Foody, CEO of Mercor, explains how Reinforcement Learning (RL) environments—comprising worlds, apps, and tasks—are replacing low-skilled crowdsourced data to train agentic AI models. He highlights how this technology is shifting from frontier labs to application-layer companies, which are building proprietary datasets as their primary competitive moat.

Evolution of AI Training Data

  • Shift to Agentic Data: The industry transitioned away from 2020-era crowdsourced behavior cloning (supervised fine-tuning and simple RLHF) toward hiring world-class experts (software engineers, lawyers, doctors, bankers) to build frontier evals and RL environments.
  • The Three Pillars of RL Environments: Brendan Foody defines an RL environment as consisting of:
    • Worlds: Messages, slides, docs, sheets, and files that mirror real-world projects.
    • Apps: High-fidelity clones of popular tools like Salesforce, ServiceNow, and Microsoft 365, accessed via MCP, CLI, or Kua.
    • Tasks: Prompts paired with rubric-based verifiers or unit tests for evaluation and training.
  • Scaling Expert Networks: Mercor’s expert talent network scaled to 2.5 million expert hours in Q2 alone, driven by the massive distribution of jobs in the Bureau of Labor Statistics (GDP eval) that require specialized app scenarios and tasks.

Why Humans Remain Essential

  • Measuring Beyond the Frontier: Humans are necessary to create rubrics because models struggle to grade their own complex work (e.g., slide decks or legal memos) and cannot reliably identify their own mistakes without a professor-like rubric.
  • Solving Verifier Complexity: Crafting comprehensive verifiers requires understanding vast problem spaces and dozens of potential errors. Mercor uses a process called trajectory analysis (rolling out 10 to 100 trajectories with agentic quality control and human review) to ensure auto-grader scores align with human judgment.
  • Nuanced Synthetic Data: While models act as helpful co-pilots for experts populating environments and writing outlines, humans are indispensable for setting the ground truth and avoiding noisy reward hacking.

Real-World Legal Environment & Post-Training Results

  • Apex Agents Legal Environment: Built in collaboration with lawyers from top firms like Latham and Watkins, this environment simulates a real big-law project with a comprehensive data room rendered into Google Workspace clones.
  • Task Example: Evaluating maximum total liability for *Star Tanker Tankers International Limited* versus *Cooper Jefferies Energy Corporation* under the Oil and Petroleum Act.
  • Dramatic Performance Jumps: Post-training GLM 47 on just 1,800 tasks with about $500k in compute yielded massive gains:
    • Corporate law performance jumped from 4.7% to 26.6%.
    • Generalization was observed across GDP eval and Apex V1 even without data rooms.

Data Pricing and Curation Models

  • Pricing Strategies: Mercor prices data based on customer value (target model improvement on leaderboards) and cost structures (paying experts $150/hour, resulting in cost bases ranging from $50 to $10,000 per task).
  • Three Data Curation Options:
    • Custom Tasks: Bespoke data creation where customers pay $2,000 per task (some frontier labs buy 50,000 tasks a month; tasks can take humans anywhere from a few hours to a month to complete).
    • Off-the-Shelf Data: Pre-built datasets funded by Mercor's multi-hundred-million-dollar investments, favored by neo-labs.
    • Hourly Experts: Direct organization of talent networks (used historically by companies like Harvey).

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Adoption Timeline: Brendan Foody predicts that over the next 12 months, dozens of application-layer companies will build their own industry-leading models and own their intelligence, mirroring Cursor's trajectory.
  • Horizons and Co-workers: Future RL environments will shift toward ultra-long-horizon tasks taking a human 100 to 1,000 hours (up from current sub-10-hour limits) and introduce virtual co-workers to bridge the realism gap in social and multi-agent workplace interactions.

Notable Quotes

"The barrier for frontier labs to automate everything that you can do on your laptop using Claude is how do they cover the full distribution of all of the worlds, all of the apps, and all of the tasks in the economy." — Brendan Foody
"It's as if you would be asking a human to grade their own homework. And that's why it's really valuable to have a human create a rubric similar to how a professor would create a rubric to grade an essay." — Brendan Foody
I think you guys grew from a 1 to a 2 billion dollar revenue run rate in the last 4 months or so. This company is off to the races, and I think you were just so front and center to how companies are thinking about post-training their own models and building their own intelligence. So, thank you for joining us for this conversation. Format-wise, what we're going to do is we've got 15 minutes or so of content from Brendan. He's going to talk about RL environments in particular, which I think is a new frontier topic. It'll be fun to explore. And then we're going to leave 15 minutes or so at the end for Q&A. Again, please keep your questions in your back pocket. I will turn it over to you, Brendan. Sweet. So, I'll be talking about RL environments. Starting out, I figured it's helpful to give a little bit of the background on the history of the data market and how that history ties into Mercor's origin story. Things really started in 2020 in the era of crowdsourcing data for behavior cloning. This was mainly supervised fine-tuning data—inputs and outputs—and RLHF data where you would have an annotator select from a couple of model responses which they preferred. We were able to make all this progress in fine-tuning GPT-3, making progress towards ChatGPT and GPT-4 in the crowdsourcing era of agentic data. But what we saw changing in the market, especially as we headed into 2024, was this giant transition away from the low-skilled crowdsourcing era of behavior cloning data and moving towards the agentic era of data. We moved toward how we find the highest-skilled experts in the world that can work collaboratively in teams to build frontier evals and RL environments for the next generation of models. These are all the software engineers, lawyers, doctors, bankers, et cetera, that could measure the frontier of intelligence and help to use that to improve model capabilities. Mercor grew up with our first big project being deep research. I guess that was the first prominent RL agent scaling up dramatically with all of the frontier labs to become the primary agentic data vendor to all of the leading labs and also all of the leading application layer companies, ranging from Harvey and Cera to Cognition and Ramp. What's been really exciting over the last 12 months, especially, is how RLVR within the agentic data paradigm has evolved to also include RL environments with these rich apps and worlds that teach agents how to use all of the tools on our laptops that we use every day. So, I'll be talking about that and, of course, how this technology that started in the frontier labs is now getting disseminated to the application layer and all of the products that all of you are building as you work on your companies. High-level, an RL environment includes three parts. The first part is the worlds. This includes all of the messages, slides, docs, sheets, etc., that correspond to everything you would have in a real project or company that you're working on. The second part is the apps, which are high-fidelity clones of popular applications—Salesforce, ServiceNow, Microsoft 365, etc.—that agents can interact with via MCP, CLI, or Kua. The third part is the tasks, where we have prompts and verifiers. Verifiers could be rubrics or unit tests that can be used either for eval or training. The barrier for frontier labs to automate everything that you can do on your laptop using Claude is how they cover the full distribution of all the worlds, all the apps, and all the tasks in the economy. There has been this enormous scale-out in order to do that, where humans have been really central to how we build these environments, obviously with models in the loop meaningfully. I put a graph here of the amount of expert hours of throughput from our talent network over the last 24 months, and it's a pretty crazy trajectory with respect to 2.5 million hours in Q2 alone, with growth sort of accelerating on the amount of expert time used to build out all of these environments. The reason, of course, as I mentioned, is that we need to scale out the environment distribution across every category in the economy. Many of you might know GDP eval, where there are 205 domains in the Bureau of Labor Statistics across all the different jobs, but then you have to think through how we have all of the apps corresponding to all of those jobs, all the different scenarios, and all the tasks. It's an enormous build-out. Only humans can measure the frontier in most domains, though there are rare exceptions like math, where you have a really clean simulation environment and the model is able to learn from whether it got the right answer. But in most domains, like building a slide deck, the model has an incredibly hard time identifying reliably where it made its own mistake. It's as if you were asking a human to grade their own homework. That's why it's really valuable to have a human create a rubric, similar to how a professor would create a rubric to grade an essay or a TA would grade that slide deck. Similar to the way that a lot of us learn, it is in large part from the feedback we get from those around us rather than purely plugging things into a calculator or clean simulation. Building these verifiers is hard because anytime you're building a slide deck, you need to understand the full problem space of what the 10 different slide decks are that could be a good path to go down, what the dozens of mistakes are that you could possibly make, and how to build a comprehensive verifier that captures this full solution area of what's possible. I'll walk through a sample RL environment to break this down for all of you. Part of the reason that this is so cool, which I'll get to in a moment, is that we developed a lot of this technology in collaboration with the labs. These are ones that we have open-sourced and published to the world, but now that's all starting to get disseminated to the application layer companies that are building and owning their own intelligence. They are realizing that the three core pillars of their AI strategy are their compute, their algorithms or researchers, and the data sets they build. Data is often the most differentiating factor. This is one that we published as a legal environment, where we have lawyers from top law firms like Latham and Watkins write out a scenario of a real project that they worked on in their big law job. Then they create a full outline for a data room that corresponds to all of the different messages, emails, files, and sizes of files. I cut off the full data room because it is very extensive. Of course, there's a lot of model in the loop with how they effectively populate this. Similar to how a software engineer now should not be coding by hand entirely themselves—they should probably be orchestrating agents in how to do this very productively—we render that data room into the apps, the clones of Google Workspace you can see in this scenario, and have prompts to roll out model trajectories against this. In this one, it's evaluating the maximum total liability for Star Tanker Tankers International Limited compared to Cooper Jefferies Energy Corporation under the Oil and Petroleum Act, considering all of the context from this real scenario in the data room. Similar to how a professor would create a rubric to grade an essay, they have these key rubric criteria that correspond to what the characteristics of an accurate model response are. Making sure that these rubric criteria avoid reward hacking and effectively align with the results—when you roll out 100 trajectories, making sure all of those scores are accurate—is incredibly technically challenging. There's an enormous amount of research, agentic quality control, and training on the data that goes into how you solve that problem and ultimately produce these high-quality verifiers and leaderboards that give you an aggregate model score across how well all of the different models are doing on a particular domain. As we can see, one of the big changes over the last few months is that GLM 52 and Chimera K3 are on the leaderboard. That is a huge opportunity for all of you because that gives us the foundation to actually achieve frontier intelligence in all of the specific applications and verticals that you're focusing on; that's not too far away. To give a little bit of context on what that looks like, I'll share an example of post-training on Apex Agents, which is the data set that I showed before, where we have 1,800 tasks in this example. This was a post-training run of GLM 47, but we're redoing a lot of them for Chimera K3, so we'll have updated results for you all soon. You can see the jumps just on 1,800 tasks with about 500k in compute are pretty dramatic. Corporate law went from 4.7% to 26.6%, but notice that this is just the Apex Agents data set we gave it, and it actually generalized incredibly well to GDP eval and Apex V1, which doesn't have these data rooms. We are seeing even nominal gains in some other benchmarks as well. We're doing a lot of this work of working with customers like Harvey, who I know will present on stuff later, to help build out the environments corresponding to their specific domain so that they can build frontier intelligence within that. I think Andrew talked about how Cursor was a great first example of how an application layer company could build an industry-leading model that built an enormous amount of value for their customers. I believe that over the next 12 months, there are going to be dozens of examples just like that where companies own their own intelligence, and that is the key source of the moats that they're building. Josh and I talked about this the other day as well, regarding a couple of examples of ways to curate high-quality data sets. The general three that we see most, which I'm happy to talk about and send people links to, is first by task, which is the most common. People would say, "I really like this data shape of environments in law and we will pay $2,000 per task to scale this up." As an example, certain frontier labs might buy 50,000 tasks a month from us. It tends to be pretty dramatic scale. These tasks would generally be very complex. Some would even take humans up to a month to complete that given task; sometimes it would take just a few hours. These would be custom per-task pricing. The second is off-the-shelf data, where we have invested hundreds of millions of dollars in building our own data sets that we sell to multiple customers. All these new neo-labs are generally leaning more towards off-the-shelf data because it doesn't make sense for 10 different labs to all be building their own data sets. There's a lot of value in building something once that can then be applied to everyone. The final one, which we see a little bit of but is less of our focus anymore, is just providing the experts so that customers are able to organize the experts on their own in just an hourly model. We do a little bit of that—that was how Harvey got started with us, hiring some lawyers—but it generally moves towards more of these scaled offerings of data over time. That's a little bit of the background of how to build RL environments and what they are, and I'm really excited about all this technology that we have that has previously been limited to the frontier labs all making its way to all of you. Happy to answer any questions about that. Sweet. Go ahead. Hey, I'm Ali from Astro Guide. My question is kind of open-ended, but simple. How do you price data? How do you value data? There are so many different ways. I mean, the most natural would be our customers care about model improvement, right? Our customers have a given goal of wanting to be at the frontier on a given leaderboard, so we're able to work backwards from how much is that worth to them and how much should we charge per task, and how many tasks do we think would get them to that goal. When we think about a company like Nvidia, they're probably willing to pay a billion dollars to have a frontier open-source model. There's a lot of complexity in how we price all the different ingredients that go into making that happen. The other way that we price, and the lens we look at it through, is also our cost structure to make them. Of course, when we have a task that takes 10 hours of human time and we're paying the human $150 an hour, there might be a $1500 cost basis. It becomes a question of what margin we want to run on top of that based on how differentiated and frontier that specific task is. But it's a super wide range. We have tasks that range from $50 to $10,000. How do you think about data quality? You mentioned utilizing human experts to label data, but how do you compare the human-labeled data and the frontier lab's judgment data? How do you compare them from your opinion? The core spot, which is generally when people say "data quality," is that they're referring to two things. First is realism, and secondly is the accuracy of verifiers. On realism, it's just that they want to automate everything in the economy that corresponds to corporate law in this case, right? How do we make sure that this actually reflects the real distribution of what we would see in a real lawyer's environment? That's one of the reasons that experts create outlines and help to guide all the processes of the data curation. Realism of the environment, the apps, and the tasks is incredibly important, as is granularly understanding the taxonomy that drives that realism across the entire distribution. The second part relates to the other way people think about quality, which is the accuracy of the verifiers. The way that you would train one of these models is you might roll out 100 trajectories of Kimi K3 and then use this rubric to score all of those trajectories. There are so many different paths that a model can go down. You want to make sure that the way this rubric is doing the scoring is the same as if we were to just have humans stack-rank those 100 trajectories. What we do for that is a process called trajectory analysis, where we roll out 10 trajectories of the model that we're focused on improving and then score all of those. We have some combination of agentic quality control systems and some human review go through to make sure that all of the scores align with the goals. Sometimes you can also use human feedback evals or preference labels as an eval for your auto-grader. How much do you think synthetic data generation plays into all of this, especially creating these large data rooms? The fascinating thing is I think that there's been a lot of misinterpretation of what people mean when they say "synthetic data." RLVR is a bet on synthetic data. It's basically: let's roll out a bunch of synthetic model trajectories rather than having the humans write the SFT, and then let's score all of them and let the models learn from all of these synthetic model trajectories. I think that's the first way that synthetics get used. The second way is that models play a giant role in the way that we populate environments and create tasks. In the same way that a lawyer writing a legal memo should definitely be using Claude or ChatGPT to do that, the experts that are building out these data rooms should definitely be using them to help. There's a lot of ways that the model can make them more efficient. But the reason that humans are still an essential component of the process—that's incredibly differentiated—is that you need humans almost definitionally to measure what is beyond the frontier of the model's capabilities. You can't just tell the model to come up with a legal environment and then tell me which of your legal memos are good and bad. It's super noisy, and there aren't clear signals associated with that. You need something that has capabilities beyond the frontier of that model to do so reliably. Thank you for doing this. My question is: RL environments seem like they're all the rage now and maybe have been for about a year. I hadn't really been hearing about them prior to that; it was all human expert labeling. Why is it all about RL environments now? Is that the most relevant thing for application companies to be thinking about? And is there something after RL environments? I'll start with why it's become the rage and some of the differences between the deep research paradigm and the environments paradigm as we saw it in 2025. Then I'll talk about looking forward at what we see evolving in the data landscape. I think the reason the deep research environments were the first was because deep research had tool use with search. So, search was the tool in the environment that the model would work in. But the experts would not necessarily be populating apps; it was sort of a lighter version of an RL environment where they would just create rubrics corresponding to this. For the trend of apps in 2025, I think that really became giant because people realized that the primary bottleneck to making the models useful was how they started to use both all the context in the codebase and all the tools on our laptops. If we want this in the user distribution of usage, then we need to get it in the data distribution that the models are learning from. There's going to continue to be a giant scale-up of diversity across all three of these categories on a going-forward basis, but there are going to be some changes. To name two of those changes that we're thinking about the most: the first one is ultra-long horizon. Right now, agents mostly aren't trained to do things that are over 10 hours, and we need to start building tasks for things that might take a human 100 hours or even 1,000 hours. The other large shift that we're seeing is introducing virtual co-workers. One of my favorite questions to ask people when they're thinking about their data distribution is: "What percentage of tasks that they do in their job require interacting with other people?" Most people would say 60% or 70%. If you map that onto what percentage of evals measure how well the models can interact with other people, it's like 1%. There's this giant realism gap associated with how you actually measure how well agents engage in social interaction with all the different people and other agents that they need to work with in their jobs. You talked about rubric generation, which seems like a bespoke access of verify that you put to a task. From what I understood, that's bottlenecked by experts. Have you found any success in being able to scale that up with your models giving you a heuristic or even using post-training models for it? We have found that you can make it a lot more efficient if you have an AI copilot that's able to work with the expert in creating the task and the verifier. The expert can talk to the trajectory and understand exactly what's happening and where it's going wrong. The challenge is just that if you're trying to improve a model, it cannot reliably write out the rubric criteria for where it's making mistakes. It might get half of them right and half of them wrong, and that amount of noise is unworkable from a training standpoint. That's the reason that the process that requires humans the most is the task creation. A lot of the environments can use a lot of synthetic data, but with the tasks, those tend to really require humans, with rare exceptions in code or if you're distilling. If you have a model that's worse than a frontier model, it can definitely learn from tasks that the frontier model is creating. Hey, this is Nikhil from Cribl. When we think about RL environments for certain provable domains like cyber defense or incident response, where the agent is trying to find a flaw in an existing system, do you use humans for just authoring that environment or setting it up, or do you also use that for grading? Is there a way to scale that up? I actually think cyber is one where you don't necessarily always need humans for the verifiers because you can have an attacker and a defender agent. I think you're right in saying that for cyber you can have humans more so, or architect what is a realistic environment, because you do need a lot of diversity. But it's less human-intensive with respect to building verifiers. How can you tell when you're limited by the base model? What do you mean by that? Well, ostensibly you're using the same data set for all these models here and they somewhat land around the same final performance on this list. But maybe if you try a smaller model, it would land much lower. Is the cause there just parameter count? The parameter count will definitely play a role in how effectively the model does insofar as how trainable it is. I think the main thing to look at is generally the gap between the "pass at 16" and the "pass at 1." If you have a model where you roll out 16 trajectories and it gets all of them totally wrong, it's sort of hopeless that the model is going to learn from that for the most part. Maybe you roll out another 100 trajectories and it gets one of them right. The ideal case is that you have a "pass at 1" where it fails, but then at "pass at 16" it gets it right once or twice, and then the model is able to learn very effectively from that. That's generally the heuristic we would use for how strong the base model needs to be to effectively learn from a given data set. Maybe a final question really quick. What advice do you have for companies as they partner with you on data? What should they rely on themselves for as part of their RL post-training versus relying on you? What's the complementary strategy? I think this is why a giant portion of our business is custom data, where we have teams that are siloed and fully exclusive to critical customers to make sure that we build out the best data sets in the world that they own. That allows them to maintain their competitive advantage associated with this while also benefiting from all of the infrastructure that we've built. There are some companies that try to build out all of the talent network and infrastructure in-house, but if you look at what the frontier labs do and the best models do, it's a pretty good indication that there are so many economies of scale from working with a partner that has all of the infrastructure, the platform, and the talent network. Thanks for having me.

Rekordhøy kroneavkastning | Resultatfremleggelsen for første halvår 2026

Norges Bank Investment Management(127.0K)·43:18·295 views·2026-08-12
TL;DRThe Government Pension Fund Global (Oljefondet) delivered a record-high half-year krone return of 1,753 billion kroner (9.4 percent) in H1 2026, driven primarily by Asian technology and chip investments, bringing total fund value to 22,683 billion kroner.
Record-Breaking...

TL;DR

The Government Pension Fund Global (Oljefondet) delivered a record-high half-year krone return of 1,753 billion kroner (9.4 percent) in H1 2026, driven primarily by Asian technology and chip investments, bringing total fund value to 22,683 billion kroner.

Financial Performance & Fund Growth

  • Record-Breaking Returns: Total fund return reached 9.4% in H1 2026, yielding the highest half-year krone return in the fund's history at 1,753 billion kroner. Total fund value reached 22,683 billion kroner by the end of June.
  • Key Growth Drivers:
    • Equity Investments: Returned 13.0%, generating over 1,000 billion kroner, specifically propelled by telecommunication and technology, with a strong focus on Asian tech and hardware/chips.
    • Fixed Income: Fixed income investments (representing 25.8% of the fund) delivered a 0.9% return despite rising interest rates.
    • Unlisted Real Estate: Unlisted real estate (1.6% of the fund) gained 3.0%, driven by 2 percentage points from rental income alongside positive valuation adjustments.
    • Renewable Infrastructure: Unlisted renewable infrastructure (0.5% of the fund, capped at a 2% mandate limit) returned -0.2%, though current renewable electricity production now equals 10% of Norway's total energy production.
  • Currency & Inflows: Capital inflows added roughly 89-100 billion kroner, while a stronger Norwegian krone reduced the fund's measured value by 427 billion kroner.
  • Budget Impact: Under the fiscal rule (allowing up to 3% spending), the fund now covers one-fourth of the Norwegian state budget.

Market Risks & Concentration

  • Concentration Risk: The 10 largest companies now account for more than 20% of the fund, and US tech stocks represent nearly one-third of the US equity index—the highest concentration ever recorded. US exposure sits at nearly 55%.
  • Downside Scenario Planning: Stress-testing indicates that a simultaneous correction in AI optimism, global fragmentation, high debt defaults, and climate extremes could cause the fund's value to drop by up to 40%.
  • Geographic Adjustments: Holdings reports indicate complete exits from 6 smaller countries (including 4 European nations such as Latvia and Liechtenstein) driven by index shifts and delegated portfolio manager decisions.

Responsible Ownership & Governance

  • Shareholder Rights Concerns: Karine highlighted a worrying global trend of weakening shareholder rights, including diminished voting classes, restricted abilities to sue companies, and relaxed reporting requirements.
  • Voting Season Activity: During the voting season, the fund voted on roughly 76,000 proposals across approximately 7,000 companies. This included voting against corporate relocations to Texas (e.g., Archery Asian) where shareholder rights are weaker, while supporting ExxonMobil's move.
  • AI and Climate Engagement: AI tools are now regularly utilized to screen the entire portfolio of 7,000 companies for operations in conflict zones. The fund also supported climate reporting shareholder proposals, such as one directed at chipmaker Nvidia.
  • Ethical Exclusions & Re-inclusions: While the advisory committee is reviewing ethical guidelines (temporarily pausing new exclusions), previously excluded companies involved in ship-beaching have been re-included into the portfolio after successfully modernizing their practices.

Notable Quotes

*"Hver fjerde polititjenestemann eller kvinne, bilvei, batterifabrikk, hva det enn skal være, kommer nå fra dette fondet."* — Nicolai Tangen
*"Det er nemlig chips, chips, chips, chips... Sånn konsentrasjon har vi vel ikke sett før. Ikke sånn med chips."* — Nicolai Tangen & Trond Grande
*"Ingen finansformuer noensinne har vedvart; alt har forsvunnet, alltid til slutt. Og vi har vært gjennom 30 år nå med helt ekstraordinære, altså unormalt sterk utvikling i finansmarkedene."* — Nicolai Tangen
Ja, hjertelig velkommen til Arendal og hjertelig velkommen til fremleggelsen av våre tall for første halvår. Og det var altså litt av et halvår. Dere så mange av de tingene som skjedde på nyhetsfronten, men når det gjelder fondets verdi, så gikk den opp i halvåret og dette tallet her, 22 683 milliarder kroner, det er det høyeste tallet noensinne på slutten av et kvartal. Det er tre faktorer som forklarer endringen i fondets verdi. Det er den avkastningen vi får i markedet. Første halvår var den høyeste kroneavkastningen dette fondet noen gang har fått med 1753 milliarder kroner. Så er det tilflyt til fondet fremdeles, cirka 100 milliarder kroner, og så er det slik at når norske kroner styrker seg, så faller verdien slik vi regner den i norske kroner, så det var om lag minus 400 milliarder kroner. Og så er det sånn at vi har jo en handlingsregel som sier at man kan bruke opp til 3 % av fondet, men fordi fondet nå er blitt så stort, så dekker det nå en fjerdedel av statsbudsjettet. Så fondet har jo hatt en fantastisk vekst, det har doblet seg i verdi de siste 5 årene, og vi har akkurat lagt frem nå et halvårsresultat som er rekordsterkt, og da er det viktig å minne oss på at det ikke er risikofritt å ha 70 % plassert i globale aksjer som dette fondet har. Så vi kommuniserer hva kan skje hvis noen tenkte scenarioer, og ikke helt utenkelige scenarioer, kan inntreffe. Det at teknologiselskapene har gått opp, har gjort at denne såkalte konsentrasjonsrisikoen har gått opp, og konsentrasjonsrisiko, med det så mener vi at fondet er konsentrert om færre og færre selskaper. Det er jo hovedsakelig teknologi hvor det altså da er mer enn 1000 milliarder som kommer fra teknologi, men stort sett alle bransjene har på en måte utviklet seg positivt, med unntak av konsumvarer, ellers så har alt gått opp. Og hva innenfor teknologi er det som har dratt det her?
