Renter, en fjerdedel av fondet, litt mer enn det, er investert i rentepapirer. Det betyr at vi låner ut penger til stater, statlige foretak og selskaper. Ja, jeg skulle bare egentlig, det kom inn i mandatet i 21, og i begynnelsen så gjorde vi veldig lite, fordi det ikke var spesielt attraktivt. Nå er det mer attraktivt. Derfor har vi akselerert. Da skal vi snart begynne å runde av, så dere kan gå ut i solen. Har vi noen flere spørsmål? Ja, solen forsvinner jo under her. Ta vare. Pass på å være ute nå når det er sol. Vi skal på briller. Ja, da sier vi takk for oss. Tusen takk for at dere kom. Også husk på å komme tilbake til kanalplassen klokken 15:30, når vi skal ha en aldri så liten bursdagsfest for fondet som blir 30 år. Tusen takk for dere på stream. Takk.

Y Combinator CEO on Founder Psychology in the Age of AI

a16z(286.0K)·51:28·18.0K views·2026-08-12
Anish AcharyaGeneral Partner, a16z Garry TanPresident and CEO, Y Combinator
TL;DRAI is transforming company building by enabling tiny teams equipped with hundreds of agents and markdown-file "employees" to operate at a scale that once required massive organizations.
Garry Tan and Anish Acharya discuss how this shift redefines founder psychology, renders...

TL;DR

AI is transforming company building by enabling tiny teams equipped with hundreds of agents and markdown-file "employees" to operate at a scale that once required massive organizations. Garry Tan and Anish Acharya discuss how this shift redefines founder psychology, renders traditional SaaS models vulnerable, and demands a return to first-principles ambition and earnest obsession.

1. Silicon Valley Culture & Founder Psychology

  • The Cost of Chasing Trends: Garry Tan reflects on his early career mistakes in 2003—such as joining Microsoft's Windows Mobile team instead of staying with web programming or joining early Palantir founders Joe Lonsdale and Stefan Cohen—attributing those errors to chasing what was "hot" rather than following direct experience and first-principles knowledge.
  • The "Don't LARP" Ethos: Tan emphasizes that building what matters requires earnestness, courage, and true obsession rather than mimicking surface-level tech culture or following Wall Street map-making.
  • Y Combinator's Role as "Birthright for Tech": Founded by Paul Graham, Jessica Livingston, Trevor Blackwell, and Robert Morris, YC broke down traditional pedigree barriers by relying on simple applications (12 questions plus a 1-minute video) to identify global builders and bring them to San Francisco (celebrating 7,000 participants in their startup school).
  • The Loneliness of Founders: A core value of YC is providing a peer community where founders can drop the facade of "killing it" and deal honestly with the solitary burden of business failure.

2. The Economics of Company Building in the AI Era

  • The Death of Traditional SaaS: Pure per-seat, 10-20x ARR multiple SaaS models face severe vulnerability over the next 5–10 years unless they anchor deep moats in data or network effects.
  • Hyper-Leaned Agentic Companies: Founders can now build from zero to $15 million ARR in approximately four months with just 2–3 people supported by hundreds of AI agents and reusable markdown "skill files."
  • Token Maximization for CEOs: Tan notes that frontier model lab constraints on cost and compute can be bypassed by power users running local agentic frameworks (like OpenClaw or Hermes agent) with massive token windows (800k to 1 million tokens), effectively letting a founder "live in 2028 today" at a cost of $50,000 to $100,000 a year.
  • The Markdown File as an Employee: Every business process (sales, marketing, customer support, engineering QA) can be automated by turning iterative interactions into a permanent markdown file, code, and test suite run via cron jobs.

3. Organizational Design & Eliminating Bureaucracy

  • Reversing Corporate Bloat: Historical bureaucratic middle management (such as the 7-level friction Tan experienced working on Windows Mobile in Redmond) was a 100--year distraction from true "founder mode."
  • Algorithmic Conflict Management: Startups can leverage agentic systems and automated meeting-transcript parsing (as seen with Brex executive Pedro's "crab trap" setup) to surface ground truth, resolve internal friction, and eliminate emotional politics.
  • Toyota Production System Parallel: Just as Japanese auto manufacturing empowered line workers to change the assembly line because they held the most context, AI erases traditional API lines and middle-man bottlenecks, allowing the people doing the work to directly improve it.

4. Local Politics and Civic Engagement

  • Act Local to Fix Society: Tan's civic activism in San Francisco—spurred by failures around public safety, crime against elders, and attempts to remove algebra from middle schools—demonstrates that builders must engage in local governance.
  • The Success of Local Reform: Through grassroots organizing, community advocacy, and platforms like `giryslist.org`, local tech and resident coalitions successfully pushed back against ineffective policies, leading to safer neighborhoods and highly popular local leadership under Mayor Lurie.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • SaaS Horizon: Pure per-seat SaaS models will not exist in their current form within 5 to 10 years — Garry Tan.
  • The 2027 Harness Wars: The year 2027 will be defined by the "harness wars" driven by falling compute costs and consumer AI adoption — Garry Tan.

*"A markdown file is an employee. And it's an employee that will do the job perfectly every single time and it'll do it like as many times as you want."* — Garry Tan
*"We may never achieve a utopia, but it is worthy and worth it to attempt."* — Garry Tan

Talk a little bit about loops. I think there are coding loops, which are well-discussed, and now the graph thing, which is both funny and also real, but it feels like the most interesting loops are business loops. How can you actually have a loop where the output is changing the way the business itself operates? I know Tom gave this great talk about how to organize your company in that way. Talk about how YC is organizing around loops and how you're advising your companies too. Absolutely. I mess around with OpenClaude. I created GBrain, which is kind of like soda retrieval. Earlier this year, I created garyslist.org, which is sort of an agentic newsroom. I had to create really, really good RAG around personal deep research just to be able to put together some of those articles. All I did was have OpenClaude look at that repo, extract out the agentic RAG part, especially around after-the-fact graph generation, and it just ended up being soda even against Memory Palace and a bunch of other open source. What I realized is that just allows the agent to know what million tokens to keep in its head for any given task. What's interesting is there are a couple things at work right now. The frontier model labs that are letting you use ChatGPT or Claude, they're still pretty constrained on cost and pretty constrained on the amount of compute they want to give you. If you really want to token-max, you have to use something like Hermes Agent or OpenClaude. You have to tune it all the way up; you just let me load a million tokens or 800,000 tokens into any given request and have that be in your soul.md. When you do that, I think you basically get to live in 2028. It costs, I don't know, $50,000 or $100,000 a year to use the agents at full strength, but you'll feel it more or less immediately. For a CEO or a founder, it actually makes a lot of sense to do that. You have to give yourself permission to token-max in that way. What you get is you can live in 2028 today. Not only do you want to do that task, you want to skillify it. You do some feat of strength and then you turn it into a markdown file plus code plus tests that can be reused and put into a cron job. I think across the board, a markdown file is an employee. It's an employee that will do the job perfectly every single time, as many times as you want. At that point, all you're doing is take any business process that you need in your company and you do it once perfectly. The first time you try to do it, it's going to be bad, expensive, many iterations. But at the end, as long as you can tell very, very quickly and in simple ways, "Hey agent, fix this, fix that, this was wrong," the actual trace and the history out of that agent working with you, it'll actually turn that into a skill file that's perfect. Anytime it screws up in a future case, it's just a bug fix and then it's there forever. This loop you could apply to sales, marketing, customer support, everything. To everything? Yeah, to everything. GStack was basically that for engineering, right? It's a QA loop. I had to build something that works with browsers. I automated how I thought about PM, and I had an engineering skill that was like an edge manager that made sure there was unit test and end-to-end test coverage. In the end, I automated all the other things so much that all I was doing was black-box testing to verify. I was like, "Okay, even that part is interesting." Building these agentic systems today, I think that's all you're doing—seeing where the bottlenecks are and then instructing the agent to create a piece of software or a markdown file that blows away that roadblock. We have companies now that can go from zero to 15 million ARR in about four months with two or three people and a few hundred skill files. It's amazing. There's this recursive self-improvement, this hill climbing that happens. At what point does it start to fall over? Do you need error correction? Does the error compound? Does it get constrained by exception handling or human relationships? Otherwise, these companies should just be able to grow and run forever. I guess my experience is once you get a lot of markdown files or anytime you have some large amount of data, that's where provenance matters, where conflict management matters. If you have provenance, you're like, "Well, this is the more recent one, this is the one that wins." You need a cron job that's sweeping through that and making sure that things are accurate and from the right places. These are things that I think as you use the systems, you just know that you need to build these. This is actually one of the interesting things that is an advantage for founders in their 30s and 40s now. I mean, I think there are going to be as many Patrick Collisons as ever, but one mega-trend is the 35, 40, 45-year-old founder who has been around the block, built a lot of energy. Peter Steinberger is a perfect example where he's been around the block, he knows what to build. If you take that person, suddenly there's 400 of those people; you can outperform an entire department of any Mag 7. If you've had some experience in the enterprise, there are these dynamics that you can seek to mirror and explore. Most people at an enterprise would say conflict is a bad thing, but I actually think there's this productive, constructive conflict that at its best is the search for truth. These systems allow us to explore conflicting approaches and totally unbundle it from human emotion. That's actually very important because this is why decentralization in an organization or an economy works well; every idea gets tried. Do you know Pedro from Brex is like the most aggressive person I've met who does this with his exec team? He uses OpenClaude; he was one of the people who really got me into it. He walked us through the vibe around OpenClaude being too unsafe and dangerous. What he ended up doing was building an entire open-source layer which he calls "Crab Trap." That is an agent that watches all the network traffic and all the things that OpenClaude does. Brex is one of the most regulated fintechs, of course. The upshot is that he has his agent look at the meeting transcripts from all of his direct reports, and he can see that two levels down. The thing that jumped out at me is he can use meetings that he's not in to figure out what's broken, what's in the compliance team. He can walk in and say, "Actually, you're right. We're doing it your way," and then leave. That's so badass. It's one of the defining reasons why businesses fail. The business becomes too big to fit in one person's head, and this is the most profound form of having agents, memory, and retrieval. You zap the rocks more; the rocks give you raw intelligence about what's happening. It can introspect not just on the opinion of someone two levels down, but it can look at the ground truth from the data. That's clairvoyance. Every business in the world is available for you to take, right? The world is built on things that are built around human beings, and human beings can only keep seven plus or minus two things in their head at any given time. You plus an agent can keep basically three Harry Potter books in your head. You can rifle-shot more to come. This is the worst it will ever be, actually. So all of business, all of the things that people rely on, government especially, is still built on a world predicated by limited human beings. It's not ready for the world where agents can think at the speed of light. My hope is that products are going to be 10 or 100 times better; they should be a thousand times better. The invisible hand of capitalism, at that point properly done, isn't about lowering the cost of something. It should be creating these magical things. I was on the Wall Street Journal podcast recently, and the reporter kept trying to "gotcha" me. He kept saying, "Are you guys in tech trying to make a utopia?" I was like, "This is a trap." I would not go so far to say, but as I went through a bunch of examples from the startups you and I have worked on and funded, it's like, actually, maybe. It was a management technique—the Toyota Production System. If you are the person doing the work on the line, you should be allowed to change the line, because you are the person with the most context to improve your own working situation. That's why American cars really started sucking and why Japanese cars became absolutely incredible. There's this subtle, simple way that empowers the worker. It's so interesting because what's traditionally seen as a hierarchical society came up with this bottoms-up way of working which led to a 40-year generational run because of process power.
